That’s when I get wound up and start avoiding things.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually try to avoid things that make you feel wound up, like invoices and client messages, as soon as you start feeling anxious about them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I came in because ever since I tried to turn my art into a real freelance business, I keep getting wound up over invoices and client messages, then I avoid them until I'm crying at my kitchen table or forgetting normal stuff like paying a bill.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Like crying at the kitchen table, or forgetting normal things like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about the art business specifically about the financial or organizational aspects, like invoices, or is it also about the creative or performance aspects of your art?\n\nPatient: It’s more the business side… invoices, messages, keeping it all organized. The actual art doesn’t feel like the problem in the same way. It’s when I tried to make it a real freelance thing that I started getting so wound up and then avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the business side of your art or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly the business side, but it has kind of spilled over. Like I put off replying to a client, and then while trying to keep up with the art display, I missed a utility payment at home. So… it’s not everything, but it is starting to affect other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel about the business side of your art related to the fear of not doing a good job or is it more about the administrative tasks themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s more the tasks themselves, I think. The invoices, the client messages… trying to keep it all straight. I get so wound up about it that I avoid it, and then it builds until I’m upset or I forget normal things, like a bill.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s when I tried to make it a real freelance thing that I started getting so wound up and then avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the business side of your art or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly the business side, but it has kind of spilled over. Like I put off replying to a client, and then while trying to keep up with the art display, I missed a utility payment at home. So… it’s not everything, but it is starting to affect other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel about the business side of your art related to the fear of not doing a good job or is it more about the administrative tasks themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s more the tasks themselves, I think. The invoices, the client messages… trying to keep it all straight. I get so wound up about it that I avoid it, and then it builds until I’m upset or I forget normal things, like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of making mistakes with the invoices and client messages that makes you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… partly. It’s like I’m scared I’ll mess something up in an invoice or say the wrong thing to a client, and then I just avoid it. Then it piles up until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting normal things, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations outside of your art business that trigger a similar feeling of anxiety and avoidance?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the art stuff… though when I’m trying to keep up with things for a display, I start letting home things slide too. Like I put off a client reply and missed a utility payment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations, outside of invoices and client messages, that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects, no. It’s more the whole business side of the art… trying to make it “real.” The invoices and messages are the main things, and then I put them off until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting regular things, like bills.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’ve been having a hard time since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. I get really wound up about invoices and client messages, and then I put them off until it kind of falls apart—like I’m crying at the kitchen table or forgetting regular things, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not working on your art, like when you're doing everyday tasks or relaxing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed it when I’m just doing normal stuff. It’s more hard for me to say unless something sets it off, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of dealing with invoices and client messages that makes you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly that. When I think about the invoices or opening client messages, I get really wound up, and then I just avoid it until it’s kind of a mess. Like… crying at the kitchen table, or forgetting basic stuff like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel wound up or anxious when you think about other tasks or responsibilities outside of your art business, like household chores or planning social events?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, normal chores or social plans can be annoying, but it’s not the same. It’s mostly when it’s tied to the art business—like invoices or client messages. That’s when I get wound up and start avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to avoid things that make you feel wound up, like invoices and client messages, as soon as you start feeling anxious about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. Once I start getting that wound-up feeling about invoices or client messages, I just put it off. Then it piles up until I’m really upset, like crying at the kitchen table or forgetting something normal, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid things outside of your art business when they start to feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that. It’s mostly the art business stuff—when invoices or client messages start feeling like too much, I avoid them, and then it kind of spirals.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this anxious if you were doing a different type of work or business?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least not like this. It really started when I tried to make the art into an actual freelance business. The invoices and client messages are what set me off, and then I avoid them until it gets… messy. Like crying at the kitchen table, or forgetting normal things like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about the art business specifically about the financial or organizational aspects, like invoices, or is it also about the creative or performance aspects of your art?\n\nPatient: It’s more the business side… invoices, messages, keeping it all organized. The actual art doesn’t feel like the problem in the same way. It’s when I tried to make it a real freelance thing that I started getting so wound up and then avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the business side of your art or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly the business side, but it has kind of spilled over. Like I put off replying to a client, and then while trying to keep up with the art display, I missed a utility payment at home. So… it’s not everything, but it is starting to affect other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel about the business side of your art related to the fear of not doing a good job or is it more about the administrative tasks themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s more the tasks themselves, I think. The invoices, the client messages… trying to keep it all straight. I get so wound up about it that I avoid it, and then it builds until I’m upset or I forget normal things, like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of making mistakes with the invoices and client messages that makes you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… partly. It’s like I’m scared I’ll mess something up in an invoice or say the wrong thing to a client, and then I just avoid it. Then it piles up until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting normal things, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations outside of your art business that trigger a similar feeling of anxiety and avoidance?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the art stuff… though when I’m trying to keep up with things for a display, I start letting home things slide too. Like I put off a client reply and missed a utility payment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations, outside of invoices and client messages, that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects, no. It’s more the whole business side of the art… trying to make it “real.” The invoices and messages are the main things, and then I put them off until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting regular things, like bills.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about making mistakes in general, not just with invoices and client messages?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s mistakes in general. It’s more since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business… the invoices and client messages get me wound up, and then I avoid them until I’m really upset or I let normal things slip, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about invoices and client messages if someone else was handling them for you?\n\nPatient: Maybe less, yeah. I think it’s not the paper or the messages by themselves… it’s that I’m the one trying to make the art into a real business, and then I get overwhelmed and put it off. If someone else handled it, I don’t think I’d end up crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

If someone else handled it, I don’t think I’d end up crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your anxiety about invoices and client messages is because you are personally responsible for handling them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I came in because ever since I tried to turn my art into a real freelance business, I keep getting wound up over invoices and client messages, then I avoid them until I'm crying at my kitchen table or forgetting normal stuff like paying a bill.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’ve been having a hard time since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. I get really wound up about invoices and client messages, and then I put them off until it kind of falls apart—like I’m crying at the kitchen table or forgetting regular things, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not working on your art, like when you're doing everyday tasks or relaxing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed it when I’m just doing normal stuff. It’s more hard for me to say unless something sets it off, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of dealing with invoices and client messages that makes you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly that. When I think about the invoices or opening client messages, I get really wound up, and then I just avoid it until it’s kind of a mess. Like… crying at the kitchen table, or forgetting basic stuff like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel wound up or anxious when you think about other tasks or responsibilities outside of your art business, like household chores or planning social events?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, normal chores or social plans can be annoying, but it’s not the same. It’s mostly when it’s tied to the art business—like invoices or client messages. That’s when I get wound up and start avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to avoid things that make you feel wound up, like invoices and client messages, as soon as you start feeling anxious about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. Once I start getting that wound-up feeling about invoices or client messages, I just put it off. Then it piles up until I’m really upset, like crying at the kitchen table or forgetting something normal, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid things outside of your art business when they start to feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that. It’s mostly the art business stuff—when invoices or client messages start feeling like too much, I avoid them, and then it kind of spirals.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this anxious if you were doing a different type of work or business?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least not like this. It really started when I tried to make the art into an actual freelance business. The invoices and client messages are what set me off, and then I avoid them until it gets… messy. Like crying at the kitchen table, or forgetting normal things like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about the art business specifically about the financial or organizational aspects, like invoices, or is it also about the creative or performance aspects of your art?\n\nPatient: It’s more the business side… invoices, messages, keeping it all organized. The actual art doesn’t feel like the problem in the same way. It’s when I tried to make it a real freelance thing that I started getting so wound up and then avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the business side of your art or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly the business side, but it has kind of spilled over. Like I put off replying to a client, and then while trying to keep up with the art display, I missed a utility payment at home. So… it’s not everything, but it is starting to affect other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel about the business side of your art related to the fear of not doing a good job or is it more about the administrative tasks themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s more the tasks themselves, I think. The invoices, the client messages… trying to keep it all straight. I get so wound up about it that I avoid it, and then it builds until I’m upset or I forget normal things, like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of making mistakes with the invoices and client messages that makes you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… partly. It’s like I’m scared I’ll mess something up in an invoice or say the wrong thing to a client, and then I just avoid it. Then it piles up until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting normal things, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations outside of your art business that trigger a similar feeling of anxiety and avoidance?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the art stuff… though when I’m trying to keep up with things for a display, I start letting home things slide too. Like I put off a client reply and missed a utility payment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations, outside of invoices and client messages, that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects, no. It’s more the whole business side of the art… trying to make it “real.” The invoices and messages are the main things, and then I put them off until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting regular things, like bills.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about making mistakes in general, not just with invoices and client messages?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s mistakes in general. It’s more since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business… the invoices and client messages get me wound up, and then I avoid them until I’m really upset or I let normal things slip, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about invoices and client messages if someone else was handling them for you?\n\nPatient: Maybe less, yeah. I think it’s not the paper or the messages by themselves… it’s that I’m the one trying to make the art into a real business, and then I get overwhelmed and put it off. If someone else handled it, I don’t think I’d end up crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about invoices and client messages is because you are personally responsible for handling them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s that it’s on me, and it makes the whole art thing feel like this “real business” I’m supposed to manage. Then I get wound up and avoid it until it’s… too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s when I tried to make it a real freelance thing that I started getting so wound up and then avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the business side of your art or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly the business side, but it has kind of spilled over. Like I put off replying to a client, and then while trying to keep up with the art display, I missed a utility payment at home. So… it’s not everything, but it is starting to affect other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel about the business side of your art related to the fear of not doing a good job or is it more about the administrative tasks themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s more the tasks themselves, I think. The invoices, the client messages… trying to keep it all straight. I get so wound up about it that I avoid it, and then it builds until I’m upset or I forget normal things, like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of making mistakes with the invoices and client messages that makes you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… partly. It’s like I’m scared I’ll mess something up in an invoice or say the wrong thing to a client, and then I just avoid it. Then it piles up until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting normal things, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations outside of your art business that trigger a similar feeling of anxiety and avoidance?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the art stuff… though when I’m trying to keep up with things for a display, I start letting home things slide too. Like I put off a client reply and missed a utility payment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations, outside of invoices and client messages, that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects, no. It’s more the whole business side of the art… trying to make it “real.” The invoices and messages are the main things, and then I put them off until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting regular things, like bills.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about making mistakes in general, not just with invoices and client messages?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s mistakes in general. It’s more since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business… the invoices and client messages get me wound up, and then I avoid them until I’m really upset or I let normal things slip, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about invoices and client messages if someone else was handling them for you?\n\nPatient: Maybe less, yeah. I think it’s not the paper or the messages by themselves… it’s that I’m the one trying to make the art into a real business, and then I get overwhelmed and put it off. If someone else handled it, I don’t think I’d end up crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about invoices and client messages is because you are personally responsible for handling them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s that it’s on me, and it makes the whole art thing feel like this “real business” I’m supposed to manage. Then I get wound up and avoid it until it’s… too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about the business side of your art if you had a manager or someone else to oversee the invoices and client messages for you?\n\nPatient: I think it would help, yeah. The part that gets me wound up is having to handle the invoices and client messages myself, like it makes the art feel like this whole business I’m supposed to manage. If someone else oversaw that, I don’t think I’d get to the point of avoiding it and ending up crying at the kitchen table.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’ve been having a hard time since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. I get really wound up about invoices and client messages, and then I put them off until it kind of falls apart—like I’m crying at the kitchen table or forgetting regular things, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not working on your art, like when you're doing everyday tasks or relaxing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed it when I’m just doing normal stuff. It’s more hard for me to say unless something sets it off, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of dealing with invoices and client messages that makes you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly that. When I think about the invoices or opening client messages, I get really wound up, and then I just avoid it until it’s kind of a mess. Like… crying at the kitchen table, or forgetting basic stuff like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel wound up or anxious when you think about other tasks or responsibilities outside of your art business, like household chores or planning social events?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, normal chores or social plans can be annoying, but it’s not the same. It’s mostly when it’s tied to the art business—like invoices or client messages. That’s when I get wound up and start avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to avoid things that make you feel wound up, like invoices and client messages, as soon as you start feeling anxious about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. Once I start getting that wound-up feeling about invoices or client messages, I just put it off. Then it piles up until I’m really upset, like crying at the kitchen table or forgetting something normal, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid things outside of your art business when they start to feel overwhelming?\n\nPatient: Not really outside of that. It’s mostly the art business stuff—when invoices or client messages start feeling like too much, I avoid them, and then it kind of spirals.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this anxious if you were doing a different type of work or business?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least not like this. It really started when I tried to make the art into an actual freelance business. The invoices and client messages are what set me off, and then I avoid them until it gets… messy. Like crying at the kitchen table, or forgetting normal things like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about the art business specifically about the financial or organizational aspects, like invoices, or is it also about the creative or performance aspects of your art?\n\nPatient: It’s more the business side… invoices, messages, keeping it all organized. The actual art doesn’t feel like the problem in the same way. It’s when I tried to make it a real freelance thing that I started getting so wound up and then avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the business side of your art or does it also affect other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Mostly the business side, but it has kind of spilled over. Like I put off replying to a client, and then while trying to keep up with the art display, I missed a utility payment at home. So… it’s not everything, but it is starting to affect other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel about the business side of your art related to the fear of not doing a good job or is it more about the administrative tasks themselves?\n\nPatient: It’s more the tasks themselves, I think. The invoices, the client messages… trying to keep it all straight. I get so wound up about it that I avoid it, and then it builds until I’m upset or I forget normal things, like a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of making mistakes with the invoices and client messages that makes you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… partly. It’s like I’m scared I’ll mess something up in an invoice or say the wrong thing to a client, and then I just avoid it. Then it piles up until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting normal things, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Are there any tasks or situations outside of your art business that trigger a similar feeling of anxiety and avoidance?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly tied to the art stuff… though when I’m trying to keep up with things for a display, I start letting home things slide too. Like I put off a client reply and missed a utility payment.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific objects or situations, outside of invoices and client messages, that trigger your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really specific objects, no. It’s more the whole business side of the art… trying to make it “real.” The invoices and messages are the main things, and then I put them off until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting regular things, like bills.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about making mistakes in general, not just with invoices and client messages?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s mistakes in general. It’s more since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business… the invoices and client messages get me wound up, and then I avoid them until I’m really upset or I let normal things slip, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about invoices and client messages if someone else was handling them for you?\n\nPatient: Maybe less, yeah. I think it’s not the paper or the messages by themselves… it’s that I’m the one trying to make the art into a real business, and then I get overwhelmed and put it off. If someone else handled it, I don’t think I’d end up crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about invoices and client messages is because you are personally responsible for handling them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s that it’s on me, and it makes the whole art thing feel like this “real business” I’m supposed to manage. Then I get wound up and avoid it until it’s… too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about the business side of your art if you had a manager or someone else to oversee the invoices and client messages for you?\n\nPatient: I think it would help, yeah. The part that gets me wound up is having to handle the invoices and client messages myself, like it makes the art feel like this whole business I’m supposed to manage. If someone else oversaw that, I don’t think I’d get to the point of avoiding it and ending up crying at the kitchen table.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about handling invoices and client messages or does it extend to other tasks involved in managing your art business?\n\nPatient: Mostly the invoices and client messages, I think. But… it kind of spills over into the whole “managing the business” part of my art. I get wound up, then I avoid things, and then it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table or forgetting normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s more the whole business side of the art… trying to make it “real.” The invoices and messages are the main things, and then I put them off until I’m really upset, or I start forgetting regular things, like bills.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about making mistakes in general, not just with invoices and client messages?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s mistakes in general. It’s more since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business… the invoices and client messages get me wound up, and then I avoid them until I’m really upset or I let normal things slip, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious about invoices and client messages if someone else was handling them for you?\n\nPatient: Maybe less, yeah. I think it’s not the paper or the messages by themselves… it’s that I’m the one trying to make the art into a real business, and then I get overwhelmed and put it off. If someone else handled it, I don’t think I’d end up crying at the kitchen table over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about invoices and client messages is because you are personally responsible for handling them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s that it’s on me, and it makes the whole art thing feel like this “real business” I’m supposed to manage. Then I get wound up and avoid it until it’s… too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about the business side of your art if you had a manager or someone else to oversee the invoices and client messages for you?\n\nPatient: I think it would help, yeah. The part that gets me wound up is having to handle the invoices and client messages myself, like it makes the art feel like this whole business I’m supposed to manage. If someone else oversaw that, I don’t think I’d get to the point of avoiding it and ending up crying at the kitchen table.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about handling invoices and client messages or does it extend to other tasks involved in managing your art business?\n\nPatient: Mostly the invoices and client messages, I think. But… it kind of spills over into the whole “managing the business” part of my art. I get wound up, then I avoid things, and then it’s like I’m crying at the kitchen table or forgetting normal stuff, like paying a bill.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about handling invoices and client messages?\n\nPatient: Not only those, I guess… those are the main things, but it’s really since I tried to make my art into an actual freelance business. I get wound up about that stuff, avoid it, and then it kind of builds until I’m upset at the kitchen table or forgetting normal things, like a bill.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
